The **Solar System** is the [gravitationally](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational) bound system of the [Sun](26751.md) and the masses that [orbit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit) it, most prominently its eight [planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet), of which [Earth](9228.md) is one. The Solar System is an isolated single-star [planetary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_system) (not part of a larger [star system](240963.md)) within the [Milky Way Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milky_Way_Galaxy). The system [formed about 4.6 billion years ago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_and_evolution_of_the_Solar_System) when a dense region of a [molecular cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_cloud) collapsed, creating the Sun and a [protoplanetary disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protoplanetary_disc) from which the orbiting bodies assembled.

The Sun accounts for 99.86% of the Solar System's total mass. Inside the [Sun's core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_core), hydrogen [is fused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_nucleosynthesis#Hydrogen_fusion) into [helium](13256.md), releasing energy that is emitted through the Sun's [photosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photosphere). This creates the [heliosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliosphere) and a decreasing temperature [gradient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gradient) across the Solar System.

The next [most massive objects of the system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Solar_System_objects_by_size) are the eight planets, which by definition [dominate the orbits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clearing_the_neighbourhood) they occupy. Closest to the Sun in order of increasing distance are the [terrestrial planets](48510.md) – [Mercury](19694.md), [Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus), Earth and [Mars](14640471.md). These four planets are part of the [inner Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Solar_System). Earth and Mars are the only planets that orbit within the Sun's [habitable zone](1072751.md), in which sunlight can keep surface water liquid under atmospheric pressure. Beyond the [frost line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frost_line_\(astrophysics\)) at about five [astronomical units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_unit) (AU),[^cite_note-20] are the planets of the [outer Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Solar_System): two [gas giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_giant) ([Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter) and [Saturn](44474.md)) and two [ice giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_giant) ([Uranus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranus) and [Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptune)). Jupiter and Saturn possess nearly 90% of the non-stellar mass of the Solar System.

[Objects of planetary mass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary-mass_object) that do not dominate their orbit but directly orbit the Sun are called [dwarf planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwarf_planet). The [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union)'s Minor Planet Center lists [Ceres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceres_\(dwarf_planet\)), [Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto), [Eris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eris_\(dwarf_planet\)), [Makemake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makemake), and [Haumea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haumea) as dwarf planets.[^cite_note-21] Four other [Solar System objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_System_object) are generally identified as such: [Orcus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orcus_\(dwarf_planet\)), [Quaoar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaoar), [Gonggong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonggong_\(dwarf_planet\)), and [Sedna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedna_\(dwarf_planet\)).[^cite_note-Holler_2021-22] Less massive than the dwarf planets are the vast number of [small Solar System bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Solar_System_body), such as [asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid), [comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet), [centaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaur_\(minor_planet\)), [meteoroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteoroid), and [interplanetary dust clouds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_dust_cloud).[^cite_note-23] The dwarf planet Ceres and many of these smaller bodies are located in the [asteroid belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid_belt) (between Mars's and Jupiter's orbit), while all other dwarf planets are members of populations of [trans-Neptunian objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Neptunian_object), which may be found in the [Kuiper belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuiper_belt) just outside Neptune or in the further [scattered disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scattered_disc).

Many objects in the Solar System do not orbit the Sun directly and are instead [natural satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_satellite), commonly called 'moons', of larger bodies. These can be found throughout the Solar System in sizes from planetary-mass moons at their largest to much less massive [moonlets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlet) at their smallest. The largest two moons ([Ganymede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganymede_\(moon\)) of Jupiter and [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(moon\)) of Saturn) are larger (though less massive) than the smallest planet (Mercury), while the seven most massive, which includes Earth's [Moon](19331.md), are more massive and larger than any of the dwarf planets.

Within the heliosphere, the Solar System is constantly flooded by the charged [plasma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_\(physics\)) particles of the [solar wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind), which, along with interplanetary dust, gas and [cosmic rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_rays), form an [interplanetary medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_medium) between the bodies of the Solar System. At around 70–90 AU from the Sun, the solar wind is halted by the [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium), resulting in the [heliopause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliopause_\(astronomy\)) and the border of the interplanetary medium to [interstellar space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_space). The theorized [Oort cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oort_cloud) – the source for [long-period comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-period_comet) – extends from somewhere beyond 2,000 AU to the outermost region of the Solar System. The latter reaches to the edge of the Sun's [Hill sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_sphere), at 178,000–227,000 AU (2.81–3.59 [ly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year)), where its gravitational potential becomes equal to the galactic potential.[^cite_note-e735-24] The Solar System currently moves through a cloud of interstellar medium called the [Local Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Cloud). The [closest star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearest_stars) to the Solar System, [Proxima Centauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxima_Centauri), is 269,000 AU (4.25 ly) away. Both are within the [Local Bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Bubble), a relatively small 1,000 light-years (ly) wide region of the [Milky Way](2589714.md).

## Definition

The Solar System includes the Sun and all objects that are bound to it by gravity and orbit it.[^cite_note-x579-25][^cite_note-l783-26][^cite_note-n787-27]

The [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union) describes the Solar System as all objects that are bound by the gravity of the Sun, the Sun itself, its eight planets, and the other celestial bodies which orbit it.[^cite_note-IAU_Office_of_Astronomy_for_Education_y607-28] [NASA](18426568.md) describes the solar system as including the Sun and its planetary system.[^cite_note-k349-29]

[Capitalization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitalization) of the name varies. When not used as a proper noun and written without capitalization, "solar system" may refer to either the Solar System itself or any [planetary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_system) reminiscent of the Solar System.[^cite_note-x579-25] The [International Astronomical Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Astronomical_Union), the authoritative body regarding [astronomical nomenclature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_nomenclature), specifies capitalizing the names of all individual astronomical objects but uses mixed "Solar System" and "solar system" structures in their naming guidelines document.[^cite_note-30]

## Formation and evolution

Main article: [Formation and evolution of the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_and_evolution_of_the_Solar_System)

### Past



Diagram of the early Solar System's [protoplanetary disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protoplanetary_disk), out of which Earth and other Solar System bodies formed

The Solar System formed at least 4.568 billion years ago from the gravitational collapse of a region within a large [molecular cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_cloud).[^cite_note-AgeSolarSystem-2] This initial cloud was likely several light-years across and probably birthed several stars.[^cite_note-Arizona-32] As is typical of molecular clouds, this one consisted mostly of hydrogen, with some helium, and small amounts of heavier elements [fused](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fusion) by previous generations of stars.[^cite_note-\:3-33]

As the [pre-solar nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presolar_nebula)[^cite_note-\:3-33] collapsed, [conservation of angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_of_angular_momentum) caused it to rotate faster. The center, where most of the mass collected, became increasingly hotter than the surroundings.[^cite_note-Arizona-32] As the contracting nebula spun faster, it began to flatten into a [protoplanetary disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protoplanetary_disc) with a diameter of roughly 200 AU[^cite_note-Arizona-32][^cite_note-34] and a hot, dense [protostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protostar) at the center.[^cite_note-35][^cite_note-36] The planets formed by [accretion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accretion_\(astrophysics\)) from this disc,[^cite_note-37] in which dust and gas gravitationally attracted each other, coalescing to form ever larger bodies. Hundreds of protoplanets may have existed in the early Solar System, but they either merged or were destroyed or ejected, leaving the planets, dwarf planets, and leftover [minor bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Solar_System_body).[^cite_note-bennett_8.2-38][^cite_note-39]&#x20;

In the inner Solar System, heat from the accretion process exceeded the boiling point of hydrocarbon molecules for the first million years, leading to low carbon content for the inner planets. The boundary for this process has been dubbed the **soot line**.[^cite_note-p080-40] As the Solar System disk cooled, this line moved inward and now lies within Earth's orbit around the Sun.[^cite_note-41] Material other than metals and silicates, due to their higher boiling points, could not persist in solid form. Here planets formed that are mainly rocky, which are Mercury, Venus, Earth, and Mars. Because these [refractory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refractory_\(planetary_science\)) materials only comprised a small fraction of the solar nebula, the terrestrial planets could not grow very large.[^cite_note-bennett_8.2-38]

The giant planets (Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune) formed further out, beyond the frost line, the point between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter where material is cool enough for [volatile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volatile_\(astrogeology\)) icy compounds to remain solid. The ices that formed these planets were more plentiful than the metals and silicates that formed the terrestrial inner planets, allowing them to grow massive enough to capture large atmospheres of hydrogen and [helium](13256.md), the lightest and most abundant elements.[^cite_note-bennett_8.2-38] Leftover debris that never became planets congregated in regions such as the asteroid belt, Kuiper belt, and Oort cloud.[^cite_note-bennett_8.2-38]

Within 50 million years, the pressure and density of hydrogen in the center of the protostar became great enough for it to begin [thermonuclear fusion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fusion).[^cite_note-Yi2001-42] As helium accumulates at its core, the Sun is growing brighter;[^cite_note-\:4-43] early in its main-sequence life its brightness was 70% that of what it is today.[^cite_note-44] The temperature, [reaction rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_reaction_rate), pressure, and density increased until [hydrostatic equilibrium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrostatic_equilibrium) was achieved: the thermal pressure counterbalancing the force of gravity. At this point, the Sun became a [main-sequence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_sequence) star.[^cite_note-45] Solar wind from the Sun created the [heliosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliosphere) and swept away the remaining gas and dust from the protoplanetary disc into interstellar space.[^cite_note-\:4-43]

Following the dissipation of the [protoplanetary disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protoplanetary_disk), the [Nice model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nice_model) proposes that [gravitational encounters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_assist) between planetesimals and the gas giants caused each to [migrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_migration) into different orbits. This led to dynamical instability of the entire system, which scattered the planetesimals and ultimately placed the gas giants in their current positions. During this period, the [grand tack hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_tack_hypothesis) suggests that a final inward migration of Jupiter dispersed much of the asteroid belt, leading to the [Late Heavy Bombardment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Heavy_Bombardment) of the inner planets.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47]

### Present and future

The Solar System remains in a relatively stable, slowly evolving state by following isolated, [gravitationally bound](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitationally_bound) orbits around the Sun.[^cite_note-48] Although the Solar System has been fairly stable for billions of years, it is technically [chaotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaotic_system),[^cite_note-49] and may [eventually be disrupted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stability_of_the_Solar_System). There is a small chance that another star will pass through the Solar System in the next few billion years. Although this could destabilize the system and eventually lead millions of years later to expulsion of planets, collisions of planets, or planets hitting the Sun, it would most likely leave the Solar System much as it is today.[^cite_note-50]



The current Sun compared to its peak size in the red-giant phase

The Sun's main-sequence phase, from beginning to end, will last about 10 billion years for the Sun compared to around two billion years for all other subsequent phases of the Sun's pre-[remnant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_remnant) life combined.[^cite_note-mnras386_1-51] The Solar System will remain roughly as it is known today until the hydrogen in the core of the Sun has been entirely converted to helium, which will occur roughly 5 billion years from now. This will mark the end of the Sun's main-sequence life. At that time, the core of the Sun will contract with [hydrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen) fusion occurring along a shell surrounding the inert helium, and the energy output will be greater than at present. The outer layers of the Sun will expand to roughly 260 times its current diameter, and the Sun will become a [red giant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_giant). Because of its increased surface area, the surface of the Sun will be cooler (2,600 K (4,220 °F) at its coolest) than it is on the main sequence.[^cite_note-mnras386_1-51]

The expanding Sun is expected to vaporize Mercury as well as Venus, and render Earth and Mars uninhabitable (possibly destroying Earth as well).[^cite_note-52][^cite_note-53] Eventually, the core will be hot enough for helium fusion; the Sun will burn helium for a fraction of the time it burned hydrogen in the core. The Sun is not massive enough to commence the fusion of heavier elements, and nuclear reactions in the core will dwindle. Its outer layers will be ejected into space, leaving behind a dense [white dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_dwarf), half the original mass of the Sun but only the size of Earth.[^cite_note-mnras386_1-51] The ejected outer layers may form a [planetary nebula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_nebula), returning some of the material that formed the Sun – but now enriched with [heavier elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallicity) like carbon – to the [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium).[^cite_note-54][^cite_note-55]

## General characteristics



A color enhanced photograph from the [Moon](19331.md) of a range of components of the Solar System. The three dots at the lower left are from left to right the planets [Saturn](44474.md), [Mars](14640471.md), and [Mercury](19694.md), and in the middle of the picture rises the [Sun's corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun's_corona) over the dark [limb](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/limb) of the Moon, which is from the right lit by [earthshine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthshine).

Astronomers sometimes divide the Solar System structure into separate regions. The [inner Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_Solar_System) includes Mercury, Venus, Earth, Mars, and the bodies in the [asteroid belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid_belt). The [outer Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_Solar_System) includes Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, Neptune, and the bodies in the [Kuiper belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuiper_belt).[^cite_note-56] Since the discovery of the Kuiper belt, the outermost parts of the Solar System are considered a distinct region consisting of [the objects beyond Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Neptunian_object).[^cite_note-57]

### Composition

Further information: [List of Solar System objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Solar_System_objects) and [List of interstellar and circumstellar molecules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_interstellar_and_circumstellar_molecules)

The principal component of the Solar System is the Sun, a [G-type main-sequence star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-type_main-sequence_star) that contains 99.86% of the system's known mass and dominates it gravitationally.[^cite_note-Woolfson_2000-58] The Sun's four largest orbiting bodies, the giant planets, account for 99% of the remaining mass, with Jupiter and Saturn together comprising more than 90%. The remaining objects of the Solar System (including the four terrestrial planets, the dwarf planets, moons, [asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid), and comets) together comprise less than 0.002% of the Solar System's total mass.[^cite_note-footnoteD-62]

The Sun is composed of roughly 98% hydrogen and helium,[^cite_note-63] as are Jupiter and Saturn.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-Williams-Jupiter-65] A composition gradient exists in the Solar System, created by heat and [light pressure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_pressure) from the early Sun; those objects closer to the Sun, which are more affected by heat and light pressure, are composed of elements with high melting points. Objects farther from the Sun are composed largely of materials with lower melting points.[^cite_note-66] The boundary in the Solar System beyond which those volatile substances could coalesce is known as the [frost line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frost_line_\(astrophysics\)), and it lies at roughly five times the Earth's distance from the Sun.[^cite_note-Mumma-9]

### Orbits



Animations of the Solar System's [inner planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inner_planet) orbiting. Each frame represents 2 days of motion.



Animations of the Solar System's [outer planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outer_planet) orbiting. This animation is 100 times faster than the inner planet animation.

The planets and other large objects in orbit around the Sun lie near the [invariable plane of the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invariable_plane#Solar_System), as does Earth's orbit, known as the [ecliptic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic), and most closely the orbit of Jupiter, with an inclination to it of 0.3219°.[^cite_note-j770-67] Smaller icy objects such as comets frequently orbit at significantly greater angles to this plane.[^cite_note-Levison2003-68][^cite_note-69] Most of the planets in the Solar System have secondary systems of their own, being orbited by natural satellites called moons. All of the largest natural satellites are in [synchronous rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synchronous_rotation), with one face permanently turned toward their parent. The four giant planets have planetary rings, thin discs of tiny particles that orbit them in unison.[^cite_note-bennett_4.5-70]

As a result of the [formation of the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_and_evolution_of_the_Solar_System), planets and most other objects orbit the Sun in the same direction that the Sun is rotating. That is, counter-clockwise, as viewed from above Earth's north pole.[^cite_note-71] There are exceptions, such as [Halley's Comet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halley's_Comet).[^cite_note-72] Most of the larger moons orbit their planets in [prograde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrograde_and_prograde_motion) direction, matching the direction of planetary rotation; Neptune's moon [Triton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triton_\(moon\)) is the largest to orbit in the opposite, retrograde manner.[^cite_note-73] Most larger objects rotate around their own axes in the prograde direction relative to their orbit, though the rotation of Venus is retrograde.[^cite_note-74]

To a good first approximation, [Kepler's laws of planetary motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler's_laws_of_planetary_motion) describe the orbits of objects around the Sun.[^cite_note-\:0-75]<sup>: 433–437 </sup> These laws stipulate that each object travels along an [ellipse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellipse) with the Sun at one [focus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Focus_\(geometry\)), which causes the body's distance from the Sun to vary over the course of its year. A body's closest approach to the Sun is called its *[perihelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelion)*, whereas its most distant point from the Sun is called its *[aphelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aphelion)*.[^cite_note-\:8-76]<sup>: 9-6 </sup> With the exception of Mercury, the orbits of the planets are nearly circular, but many comets, asteroids, and Kuiper belt objects follow highly elliptical orbits. Kepler's laws only account for the influence of the Sun's gravity upon an orbiting body, not the gravitational pulls of different bodies upon each other. On a human time scale, these perturbations can be accounted for using [numerical models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Numerical_model_of_the_Solar_System),[^cite_note-\:8-76]<sup>: 9-6 </sup> but the planetary system can change chaotically over billions of years.[^cite_note-77]

The [angular momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angular_momentum) of the Solar System is a measure of the total amount of orbital and [rotational momentum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotational_momentum) possessed by all its moving components.[^cite_note-78] Although the Sun dominates the system by mass, it accounts for only about 2% of the angular momentum.[^cite_note-Marochnik1995-79][^cite_note-80] The planets, dominated by Jupiter, account for most of the rest of the angular momentum due to the combination of their mass, orbit, and distance from the Sun, with a possibly significant contribution from comets.[^cite_note-Marochnik1995-79]&#x20;

| Planet | Orbital period | Orbital velocity[^cite_note-81] |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| (Earth days) | (Earth years) |  |  |
| Mercury | 87.969 | 0.241 | 47.9 km/s (29.8 mi/s) |
| Venus | 224.701 | 0.615 | 35.0 km/s (21.7 mi/s) |
| Earth | 365.256 | 1.000 | 29.8 km/s (18.5 mi/s) |
| Mars | 686.980 | 1.881 | 24.1 km/s (15.0 mi/s) |
| Jupiter | 4,332.589 | 11.862 | 13.1 km/s (8.1 mi/s) |
| Saturn | 10,759.22 | 29.457 | 9.7 km/s (6.0 mi/s) |
| Uranus | 30,688.5 | 84.020 | 6.8 km/s (4.2 mi/s) |
| Neptune | 60,182 | 164.8 | 5.4 km/s (3.4 mi/s) |

### Distances and scales



Relative orbital distances in the Solar System visualized as a condensed rectangle

The radius of the Sun is 0.0047 AU (700,000 km; 400,000 mi).[^cite_note-arxiv1203_4898-82] Thus, the Sun occupies 0.00001% (1 part in 10<sup>7</sup>) of the volume of a sphere with a radius the size of Earth's orbit, whereas Earth's volume is roughly 1 millionth (10<sup>−6</sup>) that of the Sun. Jupiter, the largest planet, is 5.2 AU from the Sun and has a radius of 71,000 km (0.00047 AU; 44,000 mi), whereas the most distant planet, Neptune, is 30 AU from the Sun.[^cite_note-Williams-Jupiter-65][^cite_note-83]

With a few exceptions, the farther a planet or belt is from the Sun, the larger the distance between its orbit and the orbit of the next nearest object to the Sun. For example, Venus is approximately 0.33 AU farther out from the Sun than Mercury, whereas Saturn is 4.3 AU out from Jupiter, and Neptune lies 10.5 AU out from Uranus. Attempts have been made to determine a relationship between these orbital distances, like the [Titius–Bode law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titius%E2%80%93Bode_law)[^cite_note-84] and [Johannes Kepler's model](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mysterium_Cosmographicum) based on the [Platonic solids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platonic_solid),[^cite_note-85] but ongoing discoveries have invalidated these hypotheses.[^cite_note-Boss-86]

Some [Solar System models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_System_model) attempt to convey the relative scales involved in the Solar System in human terms. Some are small in scale (and may be mechanical – called [orreries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orrery)) – whereas others extend across cities or regional areas.[^cite_note-87] The largest such scale model, the [Sweden Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden_Solar_System), uses the 110-meter (361-foot) [Avicii Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicii_Arena) in Stockholm as its substitute Sun, and, following the scale, Jupiter is a 7.5-meter (25-foot) sphere at [Stockholm Arlanda Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockholm_Arlanda_Airport), 40 km (25 mi) away, whereas the farthest current object, [Sedna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/90377_Sedna), is a 10 cm (4 in) sphere in [Luleå](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lule%C3%A5), 912 km (567 mi) away.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-Sedna-89] At that scale, the distance to Proxima Centauri would be roughly 8 times further than the Moon is from Earth.

If the Sun–Neptune distance is scaled to 100 metres (330 ft), then the Sun would be about 3 cm (1.2 in) in diameter (roughly two-thirds the diameter of a golf ball), the giant planets would be all smaller than about 3 mm (0.12 in), and [Earth's diameter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_diameter) along with that of the other terrestrial planets would be smaller than a [flea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flea) (0.3 mm or 0.012 in) at this scale.[^cite_note-90]



Comparison of the distances between planets, with the white bar showing orbital variations. The size of the planets is not to scale.

### Habitability

Main article: [Planetary habitability in the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_habitability_in_the_Solar_System)



Comparison of the habitable zones of the Solar System and [TRAPPIST-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TRAPPIST-1), an ultracool red dwarf star known to have seven terrestrial planets in stable orbits around the star.



Comparison of the [habitable zones](1072751.md) for different stellar temperatures, with a sample of known exoplanets plus the Earth, Mars, and Venus. From top to bottom are an [F-type main-sequence star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F-type_main-sequence_star), a [yellow dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-type_main-sequence_star) (G-type main-sequence star), an [orange dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_dwarf) (K-type main-sequence star), a typical [red dwarf](56099.md), and an [ultra-cool dwarf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultra-cool_dwarf).

The [zone of habitability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumstellar_habitable_zone) of the Solar System is conventionally located in the inner Solar System around Earth, where water can exist in liquid form on the surfaces of planets.[^cite_note-NASA-20150407-91]

Besides solar energy, other factors that enable habitability are the Sun's [interplanetary magnetic field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_magnetic_field) and the planetary magnetic fields (for those planets that have them). These magnetic fields partially shield planets from high-energy interstellar particles called [cosmic rays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_ray). The density of cosmic rays in the [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium) and the strength of the Sun's magnetic field change on very long timescales, so the level of cosmic-ray penetration in the Solar System varies, though by how much is unknown.[^cite_note-Langner_et_al_2005-92]

Habitability in the Solar System is, however, not solely dependent on surface conditions, and furthermore the Solar environment, since there might be habitablity in potential [subsurface oceans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsurface_ocean) of various Solar System bodies,[^cite_note-93] or cloud layers of some planets, particularly Venus.[^cite_note-e387-94]

### Comparison with extrasolar systems

Analysis of *[Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kepler_\(spacecraft\))* data suggests that observed planetary systems in the Milky Way fall into three groups: "similar", which comprise planets of similar sizes similar distances apart and with highly circular orbits; "ordered", in which the masses of planets tend to increase with distance from their star, and "mixed", which show no pattern in masses whatsoever. The Solar System is an ordered system, as are 37% of observed systems. Similar systems however are the majority, comprising 59% of observed systems, while mixed systems comprise just 4%.[^cite_note-95]

Compared to many extrasolar systems, the Solar System stands out in lacking planets interior to the orbit of Mercury.[^cite_note-Martin082015-96][^cite_note-97] The known Solar System lacks any example of [super-Earths](10883868.md), planets between one and ten times as massive as the Earth that are the most common planet type in our galaxy,[^cite_note-Martin082015-96][^cite_note-98] although the hypothetical [Planet Nine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet_Nine), if it does exist, could be a super-Earth orbiting in the edge of the Solar System.[^cite_note-99] In addition, the Sun is a [flare star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flare_star) which on average shows weaker flares compared with other stars that are similar to the Sun in spectral type, rotation period and age.[^cite_note-100]

Uncommonly, the Solar System has only small terrestrial and large gas giants; elsewhere planets of intermediate size are typical – both rocky and gas – so there is no "gap" as seen between the size of Earth and of Neptune (with a radius 3.8 times as large). As many of these super-Earths are closer to their respective stars than Mercury is to the Sun, a hypothesis has arisen that all planetary systems start with many close-in planets, and that typically a sequence of their collisions causes consolidation of mass into few larger planets, but in case of the Solar System the [collisions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collision) caused their destruction and ejection.[^cite_note-Martin082015-96][^cite_note-101]

The orbits of Solar System planets are nearly circular. Compared to many other systems, they have smaller [orbital eccentricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_eccentricity).[^cite_note-Martin082015-96] Although there are attempts to explain it partly with a bias in the [radial-velocity detection method](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doppler_spectroscopy) and partly with long interactions of a quite high number of planets, the exact causes remain undetermined.[^cite_note-Martin082015-96][^cite_note-102]

## Sun

Main article: [Sun](26751.md)

White ball of plasma

The Sun in true color as viewed through a [solar filter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_filter)

The Sun is the Solar System's star and by far its most massive component. Its large mass (332,900 [Earth masses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_mass)),[^cite_note-103] which comprises 99.86% of all the mass in the Solar System,[^cite_note-Woolfson_2000-58] produces temperatures and densities in its [core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_core) high enough to sustain nuclear fusion of hydrogen into helium.[^cite_note-104] This releases an enormous amount of [energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy), mostly [radiated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiant_energy) into [space](177602.md) as [electromagnetic radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electromagnetic_radiation) peaking in [visible light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Visible_light).[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106]

Because the Sun fuses hydrogen at its core, it is a main-sequence star. More specifically, it is a [G2-type main-sequence star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-type_main-sequence_star), where the type designation refers to its [effective temperature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effective_temperature). Hotter main-sequence stars are more luminous but shorter lived. The Sun's temperature is intermediate between that of the [hottest stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O-type_main-sequence_star) and that of the coolest stars. Stars brighter and hotter than the Sun are rare, whereas substantially dimmer and cooler stars, known as [red dwarfs](56099.md), make up about 75% of the [fusor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fusor_\(astronomy\)) stars in the [Milky Way](2589714.md).[^cite_note-107]

The Sun is a [population I star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_I_star), having formed in the galaxy's [spiral arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spiral_arm). It has a higher abundance of elements heavier than hydrogen and helium ("[metals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metallicity)" in astronomical parlance) than the older population II stars in the [galactic bulge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_bulge) and [halo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_halo).[^cite_note-108] Elements heavier than hydrogen and helium were formed in the cores of ancient and exploding stars, so the first generation of stars had to die before the [universe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universe) could be enriched with these atoms. The oldest stars contain few metals, whereas stars born later have more. This higher metallicity is thought to have been crucial to the Sun's development of a [planetary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_system) because the planets formed from the accretion of "metals".[^cite_note-109]

The region of space dominated by the Solar [magnetosphere](20479.md) is the [heliosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliosphere), which spans much of the Solar System. Along with [light](27979.md), the Sun radiates a continuous stream of charged particles (a [plasma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_\(physics\))) called the [solar wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind). This stream spreads outwards at speeds from 900,000 kilometres per hour (560,000 mph) to 2,880,000 kilometres per hour (1,790,000 mph),[^cite_note-110] filling the vacuum between the bodies of the Solar System. The result is a [thin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum), dusty atmosphere, called the [interplanetary medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_medium), which extends to at least 100 AU.[^cite_note-Voyager-111]

Activity on the Sun's surface, such as [solar flares](54648.md) and [coronal mass ejections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronal_mass_ejection), disturbs the heliosphere, creating [space weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_weather) and causing [geomagnetic storms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geomagnetic_storm).[^cite_note-SunFlip-112] Coronal mass ejections and similar events blow a magnetic field and huge quantities of material from the surface of the Sun. The interaction of this magnetic field and material with Earth's magnetic field funnels charged particles into Earth's upper atmosphere, where its interactions create [aurorae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurora_\(astronomy\)) seen near the [magnetic poles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth's_magnetic_field#Magnetic_poles).[^cite_note-113] The largest stable structure within the heliosphere is the [heliospheric current sheet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliospheric_current_sheet), a spiral form created by the actions of the Sun's rotating magnetic field on the interplanetary medium.[^cite_note-114][^cite_note-115]

## Inner Solar System

The inner Solar System is the region comprising the [terrestrial planets](#Terrestrial_planets) and the [asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid).[^cite_note-116] Composed mainly of [silicates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicate) and metals,[^cite_note-117] the objects of the inner Solar System are relatively close to the Sun; the radius of this entire region is less than the distance between the orbits of Jupiter and Saturn. This region is within the [frost line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frost_line_\(astrophysics\)), which is a little less than 5 AU from the Sun.[^cite_note-Levison2003-68]

### Inner planets

Main article: [Terrestrial planet](48510.md)

Venus and Earth about the same size, Mars is about 0.55 times as big and Mercury is about 0.4 times as big

The four terrestrial planets [Mercury](19694.md), [Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus), [Earth](9228.md) and [Mars](14640471.md)

The four terrestrial or inner planets have dense, rocky compositions, few or no [moons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_satellite), and no [ring systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_ring). They are composed largely of [refractory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Refractory_\(planetary_science\)) minerals such as [silicates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silicates)—which form their [crusts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crust_\(geology\)) and [mantles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mantle_\(geology\))—and metals such as iron and nickel which form their [cores](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_core). Three of the four inner planets (Venus, Earth, and Mars) have [atmospheres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmosphere) substantial enough to generate weather; all have impact craters and [tectonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tectonics) surface features, such as [rift valleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rift_valley) and volcanoes.[^cite_note-Ryden-118]

- [Mercury](19694.md) (0.31–0.59 AU from the Sun)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;is the smallest planet in the Solar System. Its surface is grayish, with an expansive [rupes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rupes) (cliff) system generated from [thrust faults](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrust_fault) and bright [ray systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ray_system) formed by [impact event remnants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ejecta).

  [^cite_note-120]

  &#x20;The surface has widely varying temperature, with the [equatorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equator) regions ranging from −170 °C (−270 °F) at night to 420 °C (790 °F) during sunlight. In the past, Mercury was volcanically active, producing smooth [basaltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) plains similar to the Moon.

  [^cite_note-Head_et_al_1981-121]

  &#x20;It is likely that Mercury has a silicate crust and a large iron core.

  [^cite_note-122]

  [^cite_note-Margot2012-123]

  &#x20;Mercury has a very tenuous atmosphere, consisting of [solar-wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_wind) particles and ejected atoms.

  [^cite_note-124]

  &#x20;Mercury has no natural satellites.

  [^cite_note-spaceplace.nasa.gov-125]

- [Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus) (0.72–0.73 AU)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;has a reflective, whitish atmosphere that is mainly composed of [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide). At the surface, the atmospheric pressure is ninety times as dense as on Earth's sea level.

  [^cite_note-u3r1a-126]

  &#x20;Venus has a surface temperatures over 400 °C (752 °F), mainly due to the amount of [greenhouse gases](21350772.md) in the atmosphere.

  [^cite_note-127]

  &#x20;The planet lacks a protective magnetic field to protect against [stripping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_stripping) by the solar wind, which suggests that its atmosphere is sustained by volcanic activity.

  [^cite_note-128]

  &#x20;Its surface displays extensive evidence of volcanic activity with stagnant [lid tectonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lid_tectonics).

  [^cite_note-129]

  &#x20;Venus has no natural satellites.

  [^cite_note-spaceplace.nasa.gov-125]

- [Earth](9228.md) (0.98–1.02 AU)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;is the only place in the universe where [life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life) and [surface liquid water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_distribution_on_Earth) are known to exist.

  [^cite_note-life-130]

  &#x20;Earth's atmosphere contains 78% [nitrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen) and 21% [oxygen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen), which is the result of the presence of life.

  [^cite_note-handbook-131]

  [^cite_note-NYT-20131003-132]

  &#x20;The planet has a complex [climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate) and [weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weather) system, with conditions differing drastically between [climate regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_region).

  [^cite_note-climate_zones-133]

  &#x20;The solid surface of Earth is dominated by green [vegetation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetation), [deserts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hot_deserts) and white [ice sheets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_sheet).

  [^cite_note-Carlowicz_Simmon_2019-134]

  [^cite_note-Cain_2010-135]

  [^cite_note-National_Geographic_Society_2006-136]

  &#x20;Earth's surface is shaped by [plate tectonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics) that formed the continental masses.

  [^cite_note-Head_et_al_1981-121]

  &#x20;Earth's planetary [magnetosphere](20479.md) shields the surface from radiation, limiting [atmospheric stripping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_stripping) and maintaining life habitability.

  [^cite_note-137]

  - The [Moon](19331.md) is Earth's only natural satellite.

    [^cite_note-138]

    &#x20;Its diameter is one-quarter the size of Earth's.

    [^cite_note-Metzger2021-139]

    &#x20;Its surface is covered in [very fine regolith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_soil) and dominated by [impact craters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_crater).

    [^cite_note-140]

    [^cite_note-141]

    &#x20;Large dark patches on the Moon, [maria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunar_mare), are formed from past volcanic activity.

    [^cite_note-142]

    &#x20;The Moon's atmosphere is extremely thin, consisting of a [partial vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Partial_vacuum) with particle densities of under 10

    7

    &#x20;per cm

    −3

    .

    [^cite_note-143]

- [Mars](14640471.md) (1.38–1.67 AU)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;has a radius about half of that of Earth.

  [^cite_note-Seidelmann2007-144]

  &#x20;Most of the planet is red due to [iron oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron\(III\)_oxide) in Martian soil,

  [^cite_note-145]

  &#x20;and the polar regions are covered in [white ice caps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martian_polar_ice_caps) made of water and [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide).

  [^cite_note-146]

  &#x20;Mars has an atmosphere composed mostly of carbon dioxide, with surface pressure 0.6% of that of Earth, which is sufficient to support some weather phenomena.

  [^cite_note-147]

  &#x20;During the Mars year (687 Earth days), there are large surface temperature swings on the surface between −78.5 to 5.7 °C (−109.3 to 42.3 °F). The surface is peppered with volcanoes and [rift valleys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rift_valley), and has a rich collection of [minerals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mineral).

  [^cite_note-148]

  [^cite_note-ismars-149]

  &#x20;Mars has a highly [differentiated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_differentiation) internal structure, and lost its magnetosphere 4 billion years ago.

  [^cite_note-Nimmo_2005-150]

  [^cite_note-swind-151]

  &#x20;[Mars has two tiny moons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Mars):

  [^cite_note-NYT-20200725-152]

  - [Phobos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phobos_\(moon\)) is Mars's inner moon. It is a small, irregularly shaped object with a mean radius of 11 km (7 mi). Its surface is very unreflective and dominated by impact craters.

    [\[D 7\]](#cite_note-jplssd-153)

    [^cite_note-154]

    &#x20;In particular, Phobos's surface has a very large [Stickney impact crater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stickney_\(crater\)) that is roughly 4.5 km (2.8 mi) in radius.

    [^cite_note-155]

  - [Deimos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deimos_\(moon\)) is Mars's outer moon. Like Phobos, it is irregularly shaped, with a mean radius of 6 km (4 mi) and its surface reflects little light.

    [\[D 8\]](#cite_note-Horizons-Deimos-156)

    [\[D 9\]](#cite_note-JPLSSD-157)

    &#x20;However, the surface of Deimos is noticeably smoother than Phobos because the regolith partially covers the impact craters.

    [^cite_note-158]

### Asteroids

Main article: [Asteroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid)

Asteroid populations depicted: near-Earth asteroids, Earth trojans, Mars trojans, main asteroid belt, Jupiter trojans, Jupiter Greeks, Jupiter Hilda's triangle

Overview of the inner Solar System up to Jupiter's orbit

Asteroids, except for the largest, [Ceres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceres_\(dwarf_planet\)), are classified as [small Solar System bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Solar_System_bodies) and are composed mainly of [carbonaceous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon), refractory rocky and metallic minerals, with some ice.[^cite_note-159][^cite_note-160] They range from a few meters to hundreds of kilometers in size. Many asteroids are divided into [asteroid groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid_group) and [families](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid_family) based on their orbital characteristics. Some [asteroids have natural satellites that orbit them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor-planet_moon), that is, asteroids that orbit larger asteroids.[^cite_note-161]

- [Mercury-crossing asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Mercury-crossing_minor_planets) are those with [perihelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelia) within the orbit of Mercury. At least 362 are known to date, and include the closest objects to the Sun known in the Solar System.

  [^cite_note-JPLcrosserlist-162]

  &#x20;No [vulcanoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulcanoid), asteroids between the orbit of Mercury and the Sun, have been discovered.

  [^cite_note-163]

  [^cite_note-Steffl2013-164]

  &#x20;As of 2024, one asteroid has been discovered to orbit completely within Venus's orbit, [594913 ꞌAylóꞌchaxnim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/594913_%EA%9E%8CAyl%C3%B3%EA%9E%8Cchaxnim).

  [^cite_note-165]

- [Venus-crossing asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus-crossing_asteroid) are those that cross the orbit of Venus. There are 2,809 as of 2015.

  [^cite_note-jpello-166]

- [Near-Earth asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near-Earth_object) have orbits that approach relatively close to Earth's orbit,

  [^cite_note-MorbidelliAstIII-167]

  &#x20;and some of them are [potentially hazardous objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potentially_hazardous_object) because they are large enough to cause widespread damage if they collide with Earth.

  [^cite_note-CNEOS-Basics-168]

  [^cite_note-NEO-groups-169]

  &#x20;There are over 37,000 known as of 2024.

  [^cite_note-neo-jpl-stats-170]

  &#x20;A number of solar-orbiting [meteoroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteoroid) were large enough to be tracked in space before striking Earth. It is now widely accepted that collisions in the past have had a significant role in shaping the geological and biological history of Earth.

  [^cite_note-BROWN02-171]

- [Mars-crossing asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mars-crossing_asteroids) are those with perihelia above 1.3 AU which cross the orbit of Mars.

  [^cite_note-172]

  &#x20;As of 2024, NASA lists 26,182 confirmed Mars-crossing asteroids.

  [^cite_note-jpello-166]

#### Asteroid belt

Main article: [Asteroid belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid_belt)

The asteroid belt occupies a torus-shaped region between 2.3 and 3.3 AU from the Sun, which lies between the orbits of Mars and Jupiter. It is thought to be remnants from the Solar System's formation that failed to coalesce because of the gravitational interference of Jupiter.[^cite_note-173] The asteroid belt contains tens of thousands, possibly millions, of objects over one kilometer in diameter.[^cite_note-174] Despite this, the total mass of the asteroid belt is unlikely to be more than a thousandth of that of Earth.[^cite_note-Krasinsky2002-61] The asteroid belt is very sparsely populated; spacecraft routinely pass through without incident.[^cite_note-175]



The four largest asteroids: [Ceres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceres_\(dwarf_planet\)), [Vesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4_Vesta), [Pallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Pallas), [Hygiea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10_Hygiea). Only Ceres and Vesta have been visited by a spacecraft and thus have a detailed picture.

Below are the descriptions of the three largest bodies in the asteroid belt. They are all considered to be relatively intact [protoplanets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protoplanet), a precursor stage before becoming a fully-formed planet (see [List of exceptional asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_exceptional_asteroids)):[^cite_note-176][^cite_note-nasa-dawn20110329-177][^cite_note-Marsset2020-178]

- [Ceres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceres_\(dwarf_planet\)) (2.55–2.98 AU) is the only dwarf planet in the asteroid belt.

  [^cite_note-IAU-QA-179]

  &#x20;It is the largest object in the belt, with a diameter of 940 km (580 mi).

  [^cite_note-Ermakov2017-180]

  &#x20;Its surface contains a mixture of [carbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon),

  [^cite_note-Nature_12_2018-181]

  &#x20;frozen water and [hydrated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrate) minerals.

  [^cite_note-EPSC2-182]

  &#x20;There are signs of past [cryovolcanic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cryovolcano) activity, where [volatile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volatile_\(astrogeology\)) material such as water are erupted onto the surface, as seen in [surface bright spots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bright_spots_on_Ceres).

  [^cite_note-183]

  &#x20;Ceres has a very thin water vapor atmosphere, but practically speaking it is indistinguishable from a vacuum.

  [^cite_note-184]

- [Vesta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/4_Vesta) (2.15–2.57 AU) is the second-largest object in the asteroid belt.

  [^cite_note-Astronomy_&_Astrophysics-185]

  &#x20;Its fragments survive as the [Vesta asteroid family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesta_family)

  [^cite_note-planetarysociety-186]

  &#x20;and numerous [HED meteorites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HED_meteorite) found on Earth.

  [^cite_note-Vestainterior-187]

  &#x20;Vesta's surface, dominated by [basaltic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basalt) and [metamorphic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metamorphic_rock) material, has a denser composition than Ceres's.

  [^cite_note-Takeda1997-188]

  &#x20;Its surface is marked by two giant craters: [Rheasilvia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rheasilvia) and [Veneneia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veneneia_\(crater\)).

  [^cite_note-Schenk2012-189]

- [Pallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Pallas) (2.13–3.41 AU) is the third-largest object in the asteroid belt.

  [^cite_note-Astronomy_&_Astrophysics-185]

  &#x20;It has its own [Pallas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallas_family) [asteroid family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vesta_family).

  [^cite_note-planetarysociety-186]

  &#x20;Not much is known about Pallas because it has never been visited by a spacecraft,

  [^cite_note-190]

  &#x20;though its surface is predicted to be composed of silicates.

  [^cite_note-AutoCB-19-191]

[Hilda asteroids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilda_asteroid) are in a 3\:2 resonance with Jupiter; that is, they go around the Sun three times for every two Jovian orbits.[^cite_note-Barucci-192] They lie in three linked clusters between Jupiter and the main asteroid belt.

[Trojans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trojan_\(celestial_body\)) are bodies located within another body's gravitationally stable [Lagrange points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrangian_point): [L<sub>4</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point#L4_and_L5), 60° ahead in its orbit, or [L<sub>5</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lagrange_point#L4_and_L5), 60° behind in its orbit.[^cite_note-spies-193] Every planet except Mercury is known to possess at least one trojan.[^cite_note-Connors-194][^cite_note-secondUranus-195][^cite_note-CeresVestatrojans-196] The [Jupiter trojan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter_trojan) population is roughly equal to that of the asteroid belt.[^cite_note-Yoshida2005-197] After Jupiter, Neptune possesses the most confirmed trojans, at 28.[^cite_note-198]

## Outer Solar System

The outer region of the Solar System is home to the [giant planets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant_planet) and their large moons. The [centaurs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaur_\(minor_planet\)) and many [short-period comets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Short-period_comet) orbit in this region. Due to their greater distance from the Sun, the solid objects in the outer Solar System contain a higher proportion of volatiles such as water, ammonia, and methane, than planets of the inner Solar System because their lower temperatures allow these compounds to remain solid, without significant [sublimation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublimation_\(phase_transition\)).[^cite_note-bennett_8.2-38]

### Outer planets

Main article: [Giant planet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giant_planet)

Jupiter and Saturn is about 2 times bigger than Uranus and Neptune, 10 times bigger than Venus and Earth, 20 times bigger than Mars and 25 times bigger than Mercury

The outer planets [Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter), [Saturn](44474.md), [Uranus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranus) and [Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptune), compared to the inner planets Earth, Venus, Mars, and Mercury at the bottom right

The four outer planets, called giant planets or Jovian planets, collectively make up 99% of the mass orbiting the Sun.[^cite_note-footnoteD-62] All four giant planets have multiple moons and a ring system, although only Saturn's rings are easily observed from Earth.[^cite_note-Ryden-118] Jupiter and Saturn are composed mainly of gases with extremely low melting points, such as hydrogen, helium, and [neon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neon),[^cite_note-Podolak_Podolak_et_al._2000-199] hence their designation as [gas giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_giant).[^cite_note-200] Uranus and Neptune are [ice giants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_giants),[^cite_note-201] meaning they are largely composed of ['ice' in the astronomical sense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volatile_\(astrogeology\)) (chemical compounds with melting points of up to a few hundred [kelvins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kelvin)[^cite_note-Podolak_Podolak_et_al._2000-199] such as water, methane, ammonia, [hydrogen sulfide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrogen_sulfide), and [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide).[^cite_note-Podolak_Weizman_et_al._1995-202]) Icy substances comprise the majority of the satellites of the giant planets and small objects that lie beyond Neptune's orbit.[^cite_note-Podolak_Weizman_et_al._1995-202][^cite_note-zeilik-203]

- [Jupiter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jupiter) (4.95–5.46 AU)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;is the biggest and most massive planet in the Solar System. On its surface, there are orange-brown and white cloud bands moving via the principles of [atmospheric circulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_circulation), with giant storms swirling on the surface such as the [Great Red Spot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Red_Spot) and [white 'ovals'](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oval_BA). [Jupiter possesses a strong enough magnetosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetosphere_of_Jupiter) to redirect [ionizing radiation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ionizing_radiation) and cause [auroras](49658.md) on its poles.

  [^cite_note-204]

  &#x20;As of 2026, Jupiter has [115 confirmed satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Jupiter), which can roughly be sorted into three groups:&#x20;

  - The Amalthea group, consisting of [Metis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metis_\(moon\)), [Adrastea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adrastea_\(moon\)), [Amalthea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amalthea_\(moon\)), and [Thebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thebe_\(moon\)). They orbit substantially closer to Jupiter than other satellites.

    [^cite_note-205]

    &#x20;Materials from these natural satellites are the source of Jupiter's faint ring.

    [^cite_note-206]

  - The [Galilean moons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galilean_moons), consisting of [Ganymede](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganymede_\(moon\)), [Callisto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Callisto_\(moon\)), [Io](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Io_\(moon\)), and [Europa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_\(moon\)). They are the largest moons of Jupiter and exhibit planetary properties.

    [^cite_note-207]

  - Irregular satellites, consisting of substantially smaller natural satellites. They have more distant orbits than the other objects.

    [^cite_note-list-208]

- [Saturn](44474.md) (9.08–10.12 AU)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;has a distinctive visible [ring system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rings_of_Saturn) orbiting around its equator composed of small ice and rock particles. Like Jupiter, it is mostly made of hydrogen and helium.

  [^cite_note-209]

  &#x20;At its north and south poles, Saturn has peculiar [hexagon-shaped storms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturn's_hexagon) larger than the diameter of Earth. [Saturn has a magnetosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnetosphere_of_Saturn) capable of producing weak auroras. As of 2026, Saturn has [293 confirmed satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Saturn), grouped into:&#x20;

  - Ring [moonlets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moonlet) and [shepherds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shepherd_moon), which orbit inside or close to Saturn's rings. A moonlet can only partially clear out dust in its orbit,

    [^cite_note-Sremcevic2007-210]

    &#x20;while the ring shepherds are able to completely clear out dust, forming visible gaps in the rings.

    [^cite_note-Porco2005-211]

  - Inner large satellites [Mimas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimas), [Enceladus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enceladus), [Tethys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tethys_\(moon\)), and [Dione](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dione_\(moon\)). These satellites orbit within [Saturn's E ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rings_of_Saturn#E_Ring). They are composed mostly of water ice and are believed to have differentiated internal structures.

    [^cite_note-\:2-212]

  - Trojan moons [Calypso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calypso_\(moon\)) and [Telesto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telesto_\(moon\)) (trojans of Tethys), and [Helene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helene_\(moon\)) and [Polydeuces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polydeuces_\(moon\)) (trojans of Dione). These small moons share their orbits with Tethys and Dione, leading or trailing either.

    [^cite_note-Calypso-213]

    [^cite_note-Polydeuces-214]

  - Outer large satellites [Rhea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhea_\(moon\)), [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(moon\)), [Hyperion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperion_\(moon\)), and [Iapetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iapetus_\(moon\)).

    [^cite_note-\:2-212]

    &#x20;Titan is the only satellite in the Solar System to have a substantial atmosphere.

    [^cite_note-Forget2017-215]

  - Irregular satellites, consisting of substantially smaller natural satellites. They have more distant orbits than the other objects. [Phoebe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoebe_\(moon\)) is the largest irregular satellite of Saturn.

    [^cite_note-Jewitt2007-216]

- [Uranus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranus) (18.3–20.1 AU),

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;uniquely among the planets, orbits the Sun on its side with an [axial tilt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axial_tilt) >90°. This gives the planet extreme seasonal variation as each pole points alternately toward and then away from the Sun.

  [^cite_note-217]

  &#x20;Uranus's outer layer has a muted [cyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyan) color, but underneath these clouds are [many mysteries about its climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Uranus), such as unusually low [internal heat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_heat) and erratic cloud formation. As of 2026, Uranus has [29 confirmed satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Uranus), divided into three groups:&#x20;

  - Inner satellites, which orbit inside Uranus's ring system.

    [^cite_note-Esposito2002-218]

    &#x20;They are very close to each other, which suggests that their orbits are [chaotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaotic_system).

    [^cite_note-Duncan_Lissauer_1997-219]

  - Large satellites, consisting of [Titania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titania_\(moon\)), [Oberon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oberon_\(moon\)), [Umbriel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umbriel), [Ariel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ariel_\(moon\)), and [Miranda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miranda_\(moon\)).

    [^cite_note-220]

    &#x20;Most of them have roughly equal amounts of rock and ice, except Miranda, which is made primarily of ice.

    [^cite_note-Hussmann_Sohl_et_al._2006-221]

  - Irregular satellites, having more distant and eccentric orbits than the other objects.

    [^cite_note-Sheppardmoons2024-222]

- [Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptune) (29.9–30.5 AU)

  [\[D 6\]](#cite_note-nasa-factsheet-119)

  &#x20;is the furthest planet known in the Solar System. Its outer atmosphere has a slightly muted cyan color, with occasional storms on the surface that look like dark spots. Like Uranus, many atmospheric phenomena of Neptune are unexplained, such as the [thermosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thermosphere)'s abnormally high temperature or the strong tilt (47°) of its magnetosphere. As of 2026, Neptune has [16 confirmed satellites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Neptune), divided into two groups:&#x20;

  - Regular satellites, which have circular orbits that lie near Neptune's equator.

    [^cite_note-Jewitt2007-216]

  - Irregular satellites, which as the name implies, have less regular orbits. One of them, [Triton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triton_\(moon\)), is Neptune's largest moon. It is geologically active, with erupting [geysers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geyser) of nitrogen gas, and possesses a thin, cloudy nitrogen atmosphere.

    [^cite_note-Soderblom2-223]

    [^cite_note-Forget2017-215]

### Centaurs

Main article: [Centaur (small Solar System body)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centaur_\(small_Solar_System_body\))

The centaurs are icy, comet-like bodies whose [semi-major axes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-major_and_semi-minor_axes) are longer than Jupiter's and shorter than Neptune's (between 5.5 and 30 AU). These are former Kuiper belt and [scattered disc objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scattered_disc) (SDOs) that were gravitationally [perturbed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perturbation_\(astronomy\)) closer to the Sun by the outer planets, and are expected to become comets or be ejected out of the Solar System.[^cite_note-Delsanti-Beyond_The_Planets-60] While most centaurs are inactive and asteroid-like, some exhibit cometary activity, such as the first centaur discovered, [2060 Chiron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2060_Chiron), which has been classified as a comet (95P) because it develops a coma just as comets do when they approach the Sun.[^cite_note-224] The largest known centaur, [10199 Chariklo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/10199_Chariklo), has a diameter of about 250 km (160 mi) and is one of the few minor planets possessing a ring system.[^cite_note-spitzer-225][^cite_note-Braga-Ribas-2014-226]

## Trans-Neptunian region

Beyond the orbit of Neptune lies the area of the "[trans-Neptunian region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Neptunian_object)", with the doughnut-shaped Kuiper belt, home of Pluto and several other dwarf planets, and an overlapping disc of scattered objects, which is [tilted toward the plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_inclination) of the Solar System and reaches much further out than the Kuiper belt. The entire region is still [largely unexplored](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Solar_System_exploration). It appears to consist overwhelmingly of many thousands of small worlds – the largest having a diameter only a fifth that of Earth and a mass far smaller than that of the Moon – composed mainly of rock and ice. This region is sometimes described as the "third zone of the Solar System", enclosing the inner and the outer Solar System.[^cite_note-227]

### Kuiper belt

Main article: [Kuiper belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuiper_belt)



Plot of objects around the [Kuiper belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuiper_belt) and other asteroid populations. J, S, U and N denotes Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus and Neptune.



Orbit classification of Kuiper belt objects. Some clusters that are subject to [orbital resonance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_resonance) are marked.

The Kuiper belt is a great ring of debris similar to the asteroid belt, but consisting mainly of objects composed primarily of ice.[^cite_note-physical-228] It extends between 30 and 50 AU from the Sun. It is composed mainly of small Solar System bodies, although the largest few are probably large enough to be dwarf planets.[^cite_note-Grundy2019-229] There are estimated to be over 100,000 Kuiper belt objects with a diameter greater than 50 km (30 mi), but the total mass of the Kuiper belt is thought to be only a tenth or even a hundredth the mass of Earth.[^cite_note-Delsanti-Beyond_The_Planets-60] Many Kuiper belt objects have satellites,[^cite_note-230] and most have orbits that are substantially inclined (\~10°) to the plane of the ecliptic.[^cite_note-trojan-231]

The Kuiper belt can be roughly divided into the "[classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Kuiper_belt_object)" belt and the [resonant trans-Neptunian objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resonant_trans-Neptunian_object).[^cite_note-physical-228] The latter have orbits whose periods are in a simple ratio to that of Neptune: for example, going around the Sun twice for every three times that Neptune does, or once for every two. The classical belt consists of objects having no resonance with Neptune, and extends from roughly 39.4 to 47.7 AU.[^cite_note-232] Members of the classical Kuiper belt are sometimes called "cubewanos", after the first of their kind to be discovered, originally designated [1992 *QB<sub>1</sub>*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/15760_Albion), (and has since been named Albion); they are still in near primordial, low-eccentricity orbits.[^cite_note-233]

There is strong consensus among astronomers that five members of the Kuiper belt are dwarf planets.[^cite_note-Grundy2019-229][^cite_note-JWST-234] Many dwarf planet candidates are being considered, pending further data for verification.[^cite_note-Tancredi2008-235]

- [Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto) (29.7–49.3 AU) is the largest known object in the Kuiper belt. Pluto has a relatively eccentric orbit, inclined 17 degrees to the [ecliptic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecliptic_plane). Pluto has a [2\:3 resonance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_resonance) with Neptune, meaning that Pluto orbits twice around the Sun for every three Neptunian orbits. Kuiper belt objects whose orbits share this resonance are called [plutinos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutino).

  [^cite_note-Fajans_et_al_2001-236]

  &#x20;[Pluto has five moons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moons_of_Pluto): Charon, [Styx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Styx_\(moon\)), [Nix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nix_\(moon\)), [Kerberos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerberos_\(moon\)), and [Hydra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydra_\(moon\)).

  [^cite_note-237]

  - [Charon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charon_\(moon\)), the largest of Pluto's moons, is sometimes described as part of a [binary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Binary_system_\(astronomy\)) with Pluto, as the two bodies orbit a [barycenter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barycenter) of gravity above their surfaces (i.e. they appear to "orbit each other").

- [Orcus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orcus_\(dwarf_planet\)) (30.3–48.1 AU), is in the same 2\:3 orbital resonance with Neptune as Pluto, and is the largest such object after Pluto itself.

  [^cite_note-brownlargest-238]

  &#x20;Its eccentricity and inclination are similar to Pluto's, but its perihelion lies about 120° from that of Pluto. Thus, the [phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phase_\(waves\)#Phase_difference) of Orcus's orbit is opposite to Pluto's: Orcus is at aphelion (most recently in 2019) around when Pluto is at perihelion (most recently in 1989) and vice versa.

  [^cite_note-MPC2004-D15-239]

  &#x20;For this reason, it has been called the *anti-Pluto*.

  [^cite_note-MBP-240]

  [^cite_note-241]

  &#x20;It has one known moon, [Vanth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanth_\(moon\)).

  [^cite_note-IAUC8812-242]

- [Haumea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haumea) (34.6–51.6 AU) was discovered in 2005.

  [^cite_note-243]

  &#x20;It is in a temporary 7\:12 orbital resonance with Neptune.

  [^cite_note-brownlargest-238]

  &#x20;Haumea possesses a ring system, two known moons named [Hiʻiaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hi%CA%BBiaka_\(moon\)) and [Namaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namaka_\(moon\)), and rotates so quickly (once every 3.9 hours) that it is stretched into an [ellipsoid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ellipsoid). It is part of a [collisional family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collisional_family) of Kuiper belt objects that share similar orbits, which suggests a giant impact on Haumea ejected fragments into space billions of years ago.

  [^cite_note-Noviello2022-244]

- [Makemake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makemake) (38.1–52.8 AU), although smaller than Pluto, is the largest known object in the *classical* Kuiper belt (that is, a Kuiper belt object not in a confirmed resonance with Neptune). Makemake is the brightest object in the Kuiper belt after Pluto. Discovered in 2005, it was officially named in 2008.

  [^cite_note-245]

  &#x20;Its orbit is far more inclined than Pluto's, at 29°.

  [^cite_note-Buie136472-246]

  &#x20;It has one known moon, [S/2015 (136472) 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S/2015_\(136472\)_1).

  [^cite_note-ParkerA2016-247]

- [Quaoar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaoar) (41.9–45.5 AU) is the second-largest known object in the classical Kuiper belt, after Makemake. Its orbit is significantly less eccentric and inclined than those of Makemake or Haumea.

  [^cite_note-brownlargest-238]

  &#x20;It possesses a ring system and one known moon, [Weywot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weywot_\(moon\)).

  [^cite_note-Morgado2023-248]

### Scattered disc

Main article: [Scattered disc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scattered_disc)



The orbital eccentricities and inclinations of the scattered disc population compared to the classical and resonant Kuiper belt objects

The scattered disc, which overlaps the Kuiper belt but extends out to near 500 AU, is thought to be the source of short-period comets. Scattered-disc objects are believed to have been perturbed into erratic orbits by the gravitational influence of [Neptune's early outward migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_and_evolution_of_the_Solar_System#Planetary_migration). Most scattered disc objects have perihelia within the Kuiper belt but aphelia far beyond it (some more than 150 AU from the Sun). SDOs' orbits can be inclined up to 46.8° from the ecliptic plane.[^cite_note-249] Some astronomers consider the scattered disc to be merely another region of the Kuiper belt and describe scattered-disc objects as "scattered Kuiper belt objects".[^cite_note-250] Some astronomers classify centaurs as inward-scattered Kuiper belt objects along with the outward-scattered residents of the scattered disc.[^cite_note-251]

Currently, there is strong consensus among astronomers that two of the bodies in the scattered disc are dwarf planets:

- [Eris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eris_\(dwarf_planet\)) (38.3–97.5 AU) is the largest known scattered disc object and the most massive known dwarf planet. Eris's discovery contributed to a debate about the definition of a planet because it is 25% more massive than Pluto

  [^cite_note-Brown_Schaller_2007-252]

  &#x20;and about the same diameter. It has one known moon, [Dysnomia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dysnomia_\(moon\)). Like Pluto, its orbit is highly eccentric, with a perihelion of 38.2 AU (roughly Pluto's distance from the Sun) and an aphelion of 97.6 AU, and steeply inclined to the ecliptic plane at an angle of 44°.

  [^cite_note-253]

- [Gonggong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gonggong_\(dwarf_planet\)) (33.8–101.2 AU) is a dwarf planet in a comparable orbit to Eris, except that it is in a 3\:10 resonance with Neptune.

  [\[D 10\]](#cite_note-jpldata-254)

  &#x20;It has one known moon, [Xiangliu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xiangliu_\(moon\)).

  [^cite_note-Kissetal2017-255]

### Extreme trans-Neptunian objects

Main article: [Extreme trans-Neptunian object](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_trans-Neptunian_object)



The current orbits of [Sedna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/90377_Sedna), [2012 VP113](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_VP113), [Leleākūhonua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/541132_Lele%C4%81k%C5%ABhonua) (pink), and other very [distant objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ETNO) (red, brown and cyan) along with the predicted orbit of the hypothetical [Planet Nine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet_Nine) (dark blue)

Some objects in the Solar System have a very large orbit, and therefore are much less affected by the known giant planets than other minor planet populations. These bodies are called extreme trans-Neptunian objects, or ETNOs for short.[^cite_note-Sheppard-2018-256] Generally, ETNOs' [semi-major axes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semi-major_axis) are at least 150–250 AU wide.[^cite_note-Sheppard-2018-256][^cite_note-Caju_outlier-257] For example, [541132 Leleākūhonua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/541132_Lele%C4%81k%C5%ABhonua) orbits the Sun once every \~32,000 years, with a distance of 65–2000 AU from the Sun.[^cite_note-jpldata2-258]

This population is divided into three subgroups by astronomers. The [scattered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scattered_disc_object) ETNOs have [perihelia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelia) around 38–45 AU and an exceptionally high [eccentricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_eccentricity) of more than 0.85. As with the regular scattered disc objects, they were likely formed as result of [gravitational scattering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetary_migration#Gravitational_scattering) by Neptune and still interact with the giant planets. The [detached](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Detached_object) ETNOs, with perihelia approximately between 40–45 and 50–60 AU, are less affected by Neptune than the scattered ETNOs, but are still relatively close to Neptune. The [sednoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sednoid) or [inner Oort cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hills_Cloud) objects, with perihelia beyond 50–60 AU, are too far from Neptune to be strongly influenced by it.[^cite_note-Sheppard-2018-256]

Currently, there is one ETNO that is classified as a dwarf planet:

- [Sedna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedna_\(dwarf_planet\)) (76.2–937 AU) was the first extreme trans-Neptunian object to be discovered. It is a large, reddish object, and takes \~11,400 years to complete one orbit. [Mike Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_E._Brown), who discovered the object in 2003, asserts that it cannot be part of the scattered disc or the Kuiper belt because its perihelion is too distant to have been affected by Neptune's migration.

  [^cite_note-259]

  &#x20;The [sednoid population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sednoid) is named after Sedna.

  [^cite_note-Sheppard-2018-256]

Statistical variance has been observed in the orbits of some extreme trans-Neptunian objects, whose closest approaches to the Sun are mostly clustered around one sector and who display a similar orbital tilt to each other.[^cite_note-Sheppard2014-260][^cite_note-nodes-2021-261][^cite_note-nodes-2022-262] Some astronomers have suggested that this may be the result of the influence of a large planet beyond Neptune; this hypothetical planet has been termed *[Planet Nine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planet_Nine).[^cite_note-P9H2019-263]* Others credit this statistical variance to observational biases or sheer [coincidence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coincidence).[^cite_note-264]

### Oort cloud
