An [artist's impression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artist's_impression) of the [Oort cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oort_cloud), a region still well within the [sphere of influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sphere_of_influence_\(astrodynamics\)) of the Solar System, including a depiction of the much further inside [Kuiper belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuiper_belt) (inset); the sizes of objects are over-scaled for visibility.

The [Oort cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oort_cloud) is a theorized spherical shell of up to a trillion icy objects that is thought to be the source for all long-period comets,[^cite_note-\:5-265][^cite_note-\:6-266] which were originally ejected from the planetary region by gravitational interactions with the gas giants.[^cite_note-267] Oort cloud objects move very slowly, and can be perturbed by infrequent events, such as collisions, the gravitational effects of a passing star, or the [galactic tide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_tide), the [tidal force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tidal_force) exerted by the Milky Way.[^cite_note-\:5-265][^cite_note-\:6-266] No direct observation of the Oort cloud is possible with present imaging technology.[^cite_note-268] The probe that is currently nearest to the Oort cloud, *[Voyager 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyager_1)*, is expected to reach it in 300 years.[^cite_note-jpl.PIA17046-269]

The Oort cloud is theorized to surround the Solar System from potentially \~2,000 AU from the Sun to up to \~200,000 AU. Lower estimates for the radius of the Oort cloud, by contrast, do not place it farther than 50,000 AU.[^cite_note-Encrenaz_et_al_2004-270] Most of the mass is orbiting in the region between 3,000 and 100,000 AU.[^cite_note-271] The furthest known objects, such as [Comet West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet_West), have aphelia around 70,000 AU from the Sun.[^cite_note-272]

## Gravitationally unstable populations

### Meteoroids, meteors and dust

Main articles: [Meteoroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteoroid), [Interplanetary dust cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_dust_cloud), and [Cosmic dust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cosmic_dust)



The planets, zodiacal light and meteor shower (top left of image)

Solid objects smaller than one meter are usually called meteoroids and micrometeoroids (grain-sized), with the exact division between the two categories being debated over the years.[^cite_note-273] By 2017, the IAU designated any solid object having a diameter between \~30 [micrometers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micrometre) and 1 meter as meteoroids, and depreciated the micrometeoroid categorization, instead terms smaller particles simply as 'dust particles'.[^cite_note-274]

Some meteoroids formed via disintegration of comets and asteroids, while a few formed via impact debris ejected from planetary bodies. Most meteoroids are made of silicates and heavier metals like [nickel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nickel) and [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron).[^cite_note-275] When passing through the Solar System, comets produce a trail of meteoroids; it is hypothesized that this is caused either by vaporization of the comet's material or by simple breakup of dormant comets. When crossing an atmosphere, these meteoroids will produce bright streaks in the sky due to [atmospheric entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atmospheric_entry), called [meteors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteor). If a stream of meteoroids enter the atmosphere on parallel trajectories, the meteors will seemingly 'radiate' from a point in the sky, hence the phenomenon's name: [meteor shower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteor_shower).[^cite_note-276]

The inner Solar System is home to the [zodiacal dust cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interplanetary_dust_cloud), which is visible as the hazy [zodiacal light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zodiacal_light) in dark, unpolluted skies. It may be generated by collisions within the asteroid belt brought on by gravitational interactions with the planets; a more recent proposed origin is materials from planet Mars.[^cite_note-277] The outer Solar System hosts a cosmic dust cloud. It extends from about 10 AU to about 40 AU, and was probably created by collisions within the Kuiper belt.[^cite_note-278][^cite_note-279]

### Comets

Main article: [Comet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet)

At lower left, a white coma stands out against a black background. Nebulous material streams away to the top and left, slowly fading with distance.

The sparse plasma (blue) and dust (white) in the tail of [comet Hale–Bopp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet_Hale%E2%80%93Bopp) are being shaped by pressure from [solar radiation](27979.md) and the solar wind, respectively.

Comets are [small Solar System bodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Solar_System_bodies), typically only a few kilometers across, composed largely of volatile ices. They have highly eccentric orbits, generally a perihelion within the orbits of the inner planets and an aphelion far beyond Pluto. When a comet enters the inner Solar System, its proximity to the Sun causes its icy surface to [sublimate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sublimation_\(chemistry\)) and [ionise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ion), creating a [coma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coma_\(cometary\)): a long tail of gas and dust often visible to the naked eye.[^cite_note-280]

Short-period comets have orbits lasting less than two hundred years. Long-period comets have orbits lasting thousands of years. Short-period comets are thought to originate in the Kuiper belt, whereas long-period comets, such as [Hale–Bopp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comet_Hale%E2%80%93Bopp), are thought to originate in the Oort cloud. Many comet groups, such as the [Kreutz sungrazers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kreutz_sungrazer), formed from the breakup of a single parent.[^cite_note-281] Some comets with [hyperbolic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperbolic_trajectory) orbits may originate outside the Solar System, but determining their precise orbits is difficult.[^cite_note-hyperbolic-282] Old comets whose volatiles have mostly been driven out by solar warming are often categorized as asteroids.[^cite_note-283]

## Boundary region and uncertainties

See also: [List of Solar System objects by greatest aphelion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Solar_System_objects_by_greatest_aphelion)



The Solar System (left) within the [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium), with the different regions and their distances on a [logarithmic scale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logarithmic_scale)

Much of the outer reaches of the Solar System is still unknown. The region beyond 100 AU away is virtually unexplored and learning about this region of space is difficult. Study of this region depends upon inferences from those few objects whose orbits happen to be perturbed such that they fall closer to the Sun, and even then, detecting these objects has often been possible only when they happened to become bright enough to register as comets.[^cite_note-284] Many objects are yet to be discovered in the Solar System's outer region.[^cite_note-285]

The Sun's [Hill sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_sphere), its [gravitational potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravitational_potential) reaching the galactic potential, the potential of the galactic nucleus, and thus the effective range of its gravitational influence, is thought to encompass the Oort cloud,[^cite_note-p080-40][^cite_note-Littmann-286] and extend to up to 230,000 AU from the Sun.[^cite_note-Chebotarev-15]

The boundaries of the [heliosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliosphere) and of the [Hill sphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill_sphere), the Sun's gravitational potential in respect to the [interstellar medium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_medium) and the galactic gravitational potential, at the edge of the [Oort cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oort_cloud), represent the boundaries of the Solar System with the galactic environment.

### Edge of the heliosphere



Diagram of the Sun's magnetosphere and heliosheath

The Sun's [stellar-wind bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar-wind_bubble), the [heliosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliosphere), a region of space dominated by the Sun, has its boundary at the *termination shock*. Based on the Sun's [peculiar motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peculiar_motion) relative to the [local standard of rest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_standard_of_rest), this boundary is roughly 80–100 AU from the Sun upwind of the interstellar medium and roughly 200 AU from the Sun downwind.[^cite_note-fahr-287] Here the solar wind collides with the interstellar medium[^cite_note-288] and dramatically slows, condenses and becomes more turbulent, forming a great oval structure known as the [heliosheath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliosheath).[^cite_note-fahr-287]

The heliosheath has been theorized to look and behave very much like a comet's tail, extending outward for a further 40 AU on the upwind side but tailing many times that distance downwind to possibly several thousands of AU.[^cite_note-n092-289][^cite_note-290] Evidence from the *[Cassini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassini_\(spacecraft\))* and [Interstellar Boundary Explorer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstellar_Boundary_Explorer) spacecraft has suggested that it is forced into a bubble shape by the constraining action of the interstellar magnetic field,[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292] but the actual shape remains unknown.[^cite_note-293]

The shape and form of the outer edge of the heliosphere is likely affected by the [fluid dynamics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluid_dynamics) of interactions with the interstellar medium as well as [solar magnetic fields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_magnetic_field) prevailing to the south, e.g. it is bluntly shaped with the northern hemisphere extending 9 AU farther than the southern hemisphere.[^cite_note-fahr-287] The heliopause is considered the beginning of the interstellar medium.[^cite_note-Voyager-111] Beyond the heliopause, at around 230 AU, lies the [bow shock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bow_shock): a plasma "wake" left by the Sun as it travels through the Milky Way.[^cite_note-294] Large objects outside the heliopause remain gravitationally bound to the Sun, but the flow of matter in the interstellar medium homogenizes the distribution of micro-scale objects.[^cite_note-Voyager-111]

## Celestial neighborhood

Main articles: [List of nearest stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearest_stars), [List of nearest exoplanets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearest_exoplanets), and [List of nearby stellar associations and moving groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearby_stellar_associations_and_moving_groups)



Diagram of the [Local Interstellar Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Interstellar_Cloud), the [G-Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-Cloud) and surrounding stars. As of 2022, the exact position of the Solar System within the interstellar clouds remains an unresolved question in astronomy.

[^cite_note-295]

Within 10 light-years of the Sun there are relatively few stars, the closest being the triple star system [Alpha Centauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpha_Centauri), which is about 4.4 light-years away and may be in the Local Bubble's [G-Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G-Cloud).[^cite_note-296] Alpha Centauri A and B are a closely tied pair of [Sun-like stars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_analog), whereas the closest star to the Sun, the small [red dwarf](56099.md) [Proxima Centauri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxima_Centauri), orbits the pair at a distance of 0.2 light-years. In 2016, a potentially habitable [exoplanet](9763.md) was found to be orbiting Proxima Centauri, called [Proxima Centauri b](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxima_Centauri_b), the closest confirmed exoplanet to the Sun.[^cite_note-proxima_b_discovery_paper-297]

The Solar System is surrounded by the [Local Interstellar Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Interstellar_Cloud), although it is not clear if it is embedded in the Local Interstellar Cloud or if it lies just outside the cloud's edge.[^cite_note-\:1-298] Multiple other [interstellar clouds](14979.md) exist in the region within 300 light-years of the Sun, known as the [Local Bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Local_Bubble).[^cite_note-\:1-298] The latter feature is an hourglass-shaped cavity or [superbubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superbubble) in the interstellar medium roughly 300 light-years across. The bubble is suffused with high-temperature plasma, suggesting that it may be the product of several recent supernovae.[^cite_note-299]

The Local Bubble is a small superbubble compared to the neighboring wider [Radcliffe Wave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radcliffe_Wave) and *Split* linear structures (formerly [Gould Belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gould_Belt)), each of which are some thousands of light-years in length.[^cite_note-Alves_Zucker_Goodman_Speagle_2020-300] All these structures are part of the [Orion Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion_Arm), which contains most of the stars in the Milky Way that are visible to the unaided eye.[^cite_note-301]

Groups of stars form together in [star clusters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Star_cluster), before dissolving into co-moving associations. A prominent grouping that is visible to the naked eye is the [Ursa Major moving group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ursa_Major_moving_group), which is around 80 light-years away within the Local Bubble. The nearest star cluster is [Hyades](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyades_\(star_cluster\)), which lies at the edge of the Local Bubble. The closest star-forming regions are the [Corona Australis Molecular Cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corona_Australis_Molecular_Cloud), the [Rho Ophiuchi cloud complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rho_Ophiuchi_cloud_complex) and the [Taurus molecular cloud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taurus_molecular_cloud); the latter lies just beyond the Local Bubble and is part of the Radcliffe wave.[^cite_note-302]

Stellar flybys that pass within 0.8 light-years of the Sun occur roughly once every 100,000 years. The [closest well-measured approach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_nearest_stars#Distant_future_and_past_encounters) was [Scholz's Star](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholz's_Star), which approached to \~50,000 AU of the Sun some \~70 thousand years ago, likely passing through the outer Oort cloud.[^cite_note-303] There is a 1% chance every billion years that a star will pass within 100 AU of the Sun, potentially disrupting the Solar System.[^cite_note-Raymond_et_al_2024-304]

## Galactic position

See also: [Location of Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Location_of_Earth), [Galactic year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_year), and [Orbit of the Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbit_of_the_Sun)



Diagram of the [Milky Way](2589714.md), with galactic features and the relative position of the Solar System labeled.

The Solar System is located in the [Milky Way](2589714.md), a [barred spiral galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barred_spiral_galaxy) with a diameter of about 100,000 [light-years](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light-year) containing more than 100 billion stars.[^cite_note-Lang2013-305] The Sun is part of one of the Milky Way's outer spiral arms, known as the [Orion–Cygnus Arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orion%E2%80%93Cygnus_Arm) or Local Spur.[^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307] It is a member of the [thin disk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thin_disk) population of stars orbiting close to the galactic plane.[^cite_note-308]

Its speed around the center of the Milky Way is about 220 km/s, so that it completes one revolution every 240 million years.[^cite_note-Lang2013-305] This revolution is known as the Solar System's [galactic year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_year).[^cite_note-309] The [solar apex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_apex), the direction of the Sun's path through interstellar space, is near the constellation [Hercules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hercules_\(constellation\)) in the direction of the current location of the bright star [Vega](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vega).[^cite_note-310] The plane of the ecliptic lies at an angle of about 60° to the [galactic plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galactic_plane).[^cite_note-angle-16]

The Sun follows a nearly circular orbit around the [Galactic Center](373352.md) (where the [supermassive black hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermassive_black_hole) [Sagittarius A\*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sagittarius_A*) resides) at a distance of 26,660 light-years,[^cite_note-312] orbiting at roughly the same speed as that of the spiral arms.[^cite_note-astrobiology-313] If it orbited close to the center, gravitational tugs from nearby stars could perturb bodies in the [Oort cloud](#Oort_cloud) and send many comets into the inner Solar System, producing collisions with potentially catastrophic implications for life on Earth. In this scenario, the intense radiation of the Galactic Center could interfere with the development of complex life.[^cite_note-astrobiology-313]

The Solar System's location in the Milky Way is a factor in the [evolutionary history of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolutionary_history_of_life) on Earth. Spiral arms are home to a far larger concentration of [supernovae](27680.md), gravitational instabilities, and radiation that could disrupt the Solar System, but since Earth stays in the Local Spur and therefore does not pass frequently through spiral arms, this has given Earth long periods of stability for life to evolve.[^cite_note-astrobiology-313] However, according to the controversial [Shiva hypothesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shiva_hypothesis), the changing position of the Solar System relative to other parts of the Milky Way could explain periodic [extinction events](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction_events) on Earth.[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

## Discovery and exploration

Main article: [Discovery and exploration of the Solar System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_and_exploration_of_the_Solar_System)



The motion of 'lights' moving across the sky is the basis of the classical definition of planets: wandering stars.

Humanity's knowledge of the Solar System has grown incrementally over the centuries. Although the [ancient Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greece) philosopher [Aristarchus of Samos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristarchus_of_Samos) had speculated on a [heliocentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heliocentric) reordering of the cosmos, up to the [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages)–[Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance), astronomers from Europe to India believed Earth to [be stationary at the center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geocentrism) of the universe[^cite_note-316] and categorically different from the divine or ethereal objects that moved through the sky. [Nicolaus Copernicus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicolaus_Copernicus) was the first person known to have developed [a mathematically predictive heliocentric system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copernican_heliocentrism).[^cite_note-317][^cite_note-318]

Heliocentrism did not triumph immediately over geocentrism, but the work of Copernicus had its champions, notably [Johannes Kepler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johannes_Kepler). Using a heliocentric model that improved upon Copernicus by allowing orbits to be elliptical, and the precise observational data of [Tycho Brahe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tycho_Brahe), Kepler produced the *[Rudolphine Tables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolphine_Tables)*, which enabled accurate computations of the positions of the then-known planets. [Pierre Gassendi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pierre_Gassendi) used them to predict a [transit of Mercury](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_of_Mercury) in 1631, and [Jeremiah Horrocks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremiah_Horrocks) did the same for a [transit of Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transit_of_Venus) in 1639. This provided a strong vindication of heliocentrism and Kepler's elliptical orbits.[^cite_note-319][^cite_note-320]

In the 17th century, [Galileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei) publicized the use of the telescope in astronomy; he and [Simon Marius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_Marius) independently discovered that Jupiter had four satellites in orbit around it.[^cite_note-321] [Christiaan Huygens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christiaan_Huygens) followed on from these observations by discovering Saturn's moon [Titan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Titan_\(moon\)) and the shape of the [rings of Saturn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rings_of_Saturn).[^cite_note-322] In 1677, [Edmond Halley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmond_Halley) observed a transit of Mercury across the Sun, leading him to realize that observations of the [solar parallax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solar_parallax) of a planet (more ideally using the transit of Venus) could be used to [trigonometrically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trigonometry) determine the distances between Earth, [Venus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Venus), and the Sun.[^cite_note-323] Halley's friend [Isaac Newton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isaac_Newton), in his magisterial *[Principia Mathematica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophi%C3%A6_Naturalis_Principia_Mathematica)* of 1687, demonstrated that celestial bodies are not quintessentially different from Earthly ones: the same [laws of motion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton's_laws_of_motion) and of [gravity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton's_law_of_universal_gravitation) apply on Earth and in the skies.[^cite_note-\:0-75]<sup>: 142 </sup>



Solar system diagram by [Emanuel Bowen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emanuel_Bowen) in 1747, when neither Uranus, Neptune, nor the asteroid belts had yet been discovered. Orbits of planets are to scale, but the orbits of moons and the sizes of bodies are not.

The term "Solar System" entered the English language by 1704, when [John Locke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Locke) used it to refer to the Sun, planets, and comets.[^cite_note-324] In 1705, Halley realized that repeated sightings of [a comet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halley's_Comet) were of the same object, returning regularly once every 75–76 years. This was the first evidence that anything other than the planets repeatedly orbited the Sun,[^cite_note-325] though [Seneca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seneca_the_Younger) had theorized this about comets in the 1st century.[^cite_note-326] Careful observations of the 1769 transit of Venus allowed astronomers to calculate the average Earth–Sun distance as 93,726,900 miles (150,838,800 km), only 0.8% greater than the modern value.[^cite_note-327]

[Uranus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uranus), having occasionally been observed since 1690 and possibly from antiquity, was recognized to be a planet orbiting beyond Saturn by 1783.[^cite_note-328] In 1838, [Friedrich Bessel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_Bessel) successfully measured a [stellar parallax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stellar_parallax), an apparent shift in the position of a star created by Earth's motion around the Sun, providing the first direct, experimental proof of heliocentrism.[^cite_note-329] [Neptune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neptune) was identified as a planet some years later, in 1846, thanks to its gravitational pull causing a slight but detectable variation in the orbit of Uranus.[^cite_note-330] [Mercury's orbital anomaly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perihelion_precession_of_Mercury) observations led to searches for [Vulcan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vulcan_\(hypothetical_planet\)), a planet interior of Mercury, but these attempts were quashed with [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein)'s theory of [general relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_relativity) in 1915.[^cite_note-Clemence-331]

In the 20th century, humans began their space exploration around the Solar System, starting with placing [telescopes in space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Space_telescope) since the 1960s.[^cite_note-332] By 1989, all eight planets have been visited by space probes.[^cite_note-FactSheet-333] Probes have returned samples from comets[^cite_note-334] and asteroids,[^cite_note-335] as well as flown through the [Sun's corona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sun's_corona)[^cite_note-336] and visited two dwarf planets ([Pluto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pluto) and [Ceres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ceres_\(dwarf_planet\))).[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-NASA-20180907-338] To save on fuel, some space missions make use of [gravity assist maneuvers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravity_assist), such as the two [*Voyager* probes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voyager_program) accelerating when flying by planets in the outer Solar System[^cite_note-\:7-339] and the [Parker Solar Probe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parker_Solar_Probe) decelerating closer towards the Sun after its flyby of Venus.[^cite_note-340]

Humans have landed on the Moon during the [Apollo program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo_program) in the 1960s and 1970s[^cite_note-341] and are planning to return to the Moon in the 2020s with the [Artemis program](60758751.md).[^cite_note-sn-20230313-342] Discoveries in the 20th and 21st century has prompted the [redefinition of the term *planet*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Definition_of_planet) in 2006, hence the reclassification of Pluto as a dwarf planet,[^cite_note-NYT-20220118-343] and further interest in [trans-Neptunian objects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trans-Neptunian_object).[^cite_note-\:02-344]



All [active Solar System space probes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_active_Solar_System_probes) in 2026 (and a list of upcoming ones)

[^cite_note-20]: &#x20;The scale of the Solar System is sufficiently large that astronomers use a custom unit to express distances. The [astronomical unit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomical_unit), abbreviated AU, is equal to 150,000,000 km; 93,000,000 mi. This is what the distance from the Earth to the Sun would be if the planet's orbit were perfectly circular.<sup>[\[11\]](#cite_note-19)</sup>

[^cite_note-footnoteD-62]: &#x20;The mass of the Solar System excluding the Sun, Jupiter and Saturn can be determined by adding together all the calculated masses for its largest objects and using rough calculations for the masses of the Oort cloud (estimated at roughly 3 Earth masses),<sup>[\[49\]](#cite_note-59)</sup> the Kuiper belt (estimated at 0.1 Earth mass)<sup>[\[50\]](#cite_note-Delsanti-Beyond_The_Planets-60)</sup> and the asteroid belt (estimated to be 0.0005 Earth mass)<sup>[\[51\]](#cite_note-Krasinsky2002-61)</sup> for a total, rounded upwards, of \~37 Earth masses, or 8.1% of the mass in orbit around the Sun. With the combined masses of Uranus and Neptune (\~31 Earth masses) subtracted, the remaining \~6 Earth masses of material comprise 1.3% of the total orbiting mass.

[^cite_note-124]: &#x20;Domingue, Deborah L.; Koehn, Patrick L.; et al. (2009). "Mercury's Atmosphere: A Surface-Bounded Exosphere". *Space Science Reviews*. **131** (1–4): 161–186. [Bibcode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bibcode_\(identifier\)):[2007SSRv..131..161D](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/2007SSRv..131..161D). [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.1007/s11214-007-9260-9](https://doi.org/10.1007%2Fs11214-007-9260-9). [S2CID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S2CID_\(identifier\)) [121301247](https://api.semanticscholar.org/CorpusID\:121301247). The composition of Mercury's exosphere, with its abundant H and He, clearly indicates a strong solar wind source. Once solar wind plasma and particles gain access to the magnetosphere, they predominantly precipitate to the surface, where solar wind species are neutralized, thermalized, and released again into the exosphere. Moreover, bombardment of the surface by solar wind particles, especially energetic ions, contributes to ejection of neutral species from the surface into the exosphere (via "sputtering") as well as other chemical and physical surface modification processes.

[^cite_note-155]: &#x20;["Stickney Crater-Phobos"](http://www.solarviews.com/cap/mars/phobos2.htm). [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20111103010644/http://www.solarviews.com/cap/mars/phobos2.htm) from the original on 3 November 2011. Retrieved 21 April 2024. One of the most striking features of Phobos, aside from its irregular shape, is its giant crater Stickney. Because Phobos is only 28 by 20 kilometers (17 by 12 mi), it must have been nearly shattered from the force of the impact that caused the giant crater. Grooves that extend across the surface from Stickney appear to be surface fractures caused by the impact.

[Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20181112023547/https://www.britannica.com/place/Deimos-moon-of-Mars) from the original on 12 November 2018. Retrieved 21 April 2024. It thus appears smoother than Phobos because its craters lie partially buried under this loose material.
